So notorious have these Settlements of ours 
become for fabrications of the natu:e, and so 
distinctive is ihe character of the untruth which 
tas its ongin in Shanghai, that it hus:come to 
be called the Bunder,—the Shanghai Bunder. 
Every phase of our life here is liable to pro- 
duce jt. Sometimes the crop is small and in- 
frequent ; in other seasons it is abundant and of 
such rapid recurfence that it becornes bewil- 
dering Zo fatiguing to keep tally of the harvest, 
asait is gatheréd and distributed, st such 
tunes the only'quality which sdves-the Bunder 
from becoming un ineffable nuisance and thing 
of unqualified evil is its amazing variety. “The 
victim of deception is just on the point of be- 
coming jired of hearing the. changes rung on 
one-patticulur Bunder, when u fresh one, on

Langley led his unsuspecting quarry right |. . 
into the jaws of perdition, to. wit a European 
‘constable, and the luckless wight.was led 10 
g3ol to nieditate over the inequalities of this 
world, where police inspectors look like, 
‘civilians to the discomfiture of hard-working 
individuals like himself. When the case was 
called,’ it seemed, according to. the report, 
as if the Magistrate was unable to find any 
byelaw “under which the coolle ‘could be 
cbnvicted. .Given a copy of the statutes of 
the Colony, and a copy of the bye-laws, 
together with a glance at the terms of the’ 
ricksha licence, “it is safe to say that any 
ordinary layman would «quickly have found ; 
a section, under which the coolie, who was 
nothing more or less than a thief foiled of 
his prey, could be dealt with.” Inspecter 
‘Langley prosecuted with an aptness of 
illustration-and suggestion which. is seldom 
found in police’ court reports. © He 
pointed out a fact which is only too 
true that, had it been’ a civilian who 
was the victiny of the coolies importunity, 
hg,would probably have called an Indian or 
a Chinese policenan who would have be- 
lieved the coolie and“haled the uniortunate 
victim to Court ona charge of refusing to 
pay his‘fare. ‘The police force might keep 
that fact in mind. Nobody would have 
given any attention to the civilian’s story ; 
the coolie would. have mustered a crowd of 
“witnesses,” and if the alleged fare had 
escaped with a fine for altempting to de 
fraud the poor cvolie, instead of being 
charged also with assaulting the police and 
being, drunk, he amght have considered, 
himself in luck. Fortunately, it was a sen 
sible and highly: intelligent police inspector 
who was baited and the coolic is the sufferer: 
No longer will he be able to swindle strangers 
for his licence - has been cancelled, and if 
his example serves to detér other ricksha 
men from the old practice of “squeezing” 

the visitor he will ‘not_ have lived ia vain. 
Inspector Langley deserves the “thanks of 
every onc in the community for his manly 
championship of the civilian and his 
straightforward and common-sense  state- 
ment of the pitfalls which await the civilian 
at every point... :

satisfactory by the class-master, if the. vaumi= 
ber of bis attendances equals go per const. of 
the possible number of attendances in the 
classes that he has joined ; half the fee will.

cent. No registration fee will. be charged 
students who continue their course df study 
for a second yey. A. fee of $1 a, 
month will; be charged for the lirst class 
taken and one of 50 cents a month for each 
additional, class.. In’ the engineering.and | 
science classes a. deposit not exceeding. $3- 
must be made -by students who have 
the use of any instruments or apparatus of 
value.” ‘This fee will be returned to the 
student at the close of the year if the instru 
ments and apparatus have. received no dam- 
age at his hands. It is also slated that the’ 
students will be required to do home lessons. 
Surely that is a’mistake, unless. the lessons 
are very short. How can a working appren- 
tice’ or a clerk who may be’ working‘all day 
and into the night find time to write up his 
home lessons unless he steals -his master’s 
time or denies himself all recreation? A 
student attending classes every night of the 
week could not possibly do tiome lessons 
which would in any way indicale bis pro- 
gress/ he could not do himself ‘justice, .and 
to suggest that unless his home lessons are 
up to the mark he will be disqualified for a 
prize is to discourage - the plodding student 
who is chained to’ his daily toil and! give 
an unfair advantage to those who are net, 
compelled to grind. ‘their hearts away at a 
desk uvery day. As previously noted, stud- 
ents.are required to deposit $5 as a registra? 
tion fee-and pay class fees in addition. Now 
Fit cannot be gaingaid that there are many 
poor boys sin’ Hongkong, Europeans and 
Chinese ‘alike, who would be only too glad 
‘to take advantage of these evening classes,

EVENING CLASSES FOR

tage tS 

Nearly twelve months ago, we commented 
gn the action of the educational authorities 
in Shanghai in establishing evening: continua- 
tion classes for the benefit of those’ who 
desired to increase ‘Uieir “knowledge of 
literary and technical subjects. We had the 
temerity to. suggest that the Government of. 
Horigkong might he induced to follow the 
“example of the northern Settlement, in order 
that the intelligent and studious youth of 
the Colony, who had been compelled to 
relinquish their day studies, might havean 


* éducation by attending evening classes. As 
a rule, to’ Suggest , innovations’ of such a 
racter is to cry in the wilderness; but for- 
tunately Hongkong i is blessed with a Gover- 
nor at present. who is keenly alive to the 
advantages of education and. loses no occa- 
’ sion to point ont its potentialities. For 
many.months His Excellency Sir Matthew 
Nathan has had the idea in mind, and when 
the schoo! prize distributions were in flood 
in the early part of the year ‘he seldom failed | 
to refer to the proposed- inauguration of 
evening continuation classes fo be held 
under the auspices ofthe Government. ‘The 
result of His Excellency's initiative and 
energyfis now seen in the ‘prospecius pub- 
lished in this week's Garelfe,, ‘The eyening 

* continuation classes will begin on Wednes- 
day, 3rd October, and the subjecia which 
will BA taught should appeal to a wide 
section Uh the younger geibfation, There 
will be thrce Courses; co’ couimercial, engineer- 
ing, and science, arid the classes ‘and sub- 
fects to be taught are graded to. meet the

Holiday, shall be observed as a holiday by the 
Government departarents..

for stealing a cush box contaning one gold" 
watch and a finger-ring, valued at $238, and a 
promissory note worth $502, was dealt with by

yesterday. He was orderéd to be birched and

News from Wie New Territories which arrived 
in the Colony ih day isto the effect that the 
squall which swept this Colony on Thursday 
did considerable damage to the tice crop. The 
“siver rose several feet and flooded the paddy- 
fields ; huge tree's were uprooted by the winds 
and blown yards away, while the heavy rains 
did not do'any faod ta the mud huts, some of 
which are near collapsing.

blums, Chinese, Indian, and Japanese money 
and weights, partnerships, commission and 
insurance, etc. ~The teacher’ twho seeks lo 
inform «the youthful’ mind. of the causes 
which lead to the appreciation: or depregia- | 
tion of the dollar, and to explain the reason | 
why. the Singapore dollar is al a premium, 
and subsidary coinage is rejected in Hong. 
kong has a Usk before him from which even 
the ‘redoubtable W. J, Bryan might -shrink., 
Mr, Balfour has confessed that on the subject 
of bi-metallism he is as a little child, and 
how the problems of exchange are to be ex- 
pounded and explained without reference, to 
the monetary metals it is difficult to see. It 
will certainly be interesting to sean the ex: 
amination papers submitted by the students 
at the close of the session. The engineering 
course comprises practical mathematics, ap- 
plied mechanics and mechanical drawing, 
Avery ambitious programme has been drawn 
up and nn apprentice at any of the engineer. 
ing yards i in the Colony should fail {o attend. 
If only the Chinese foremen in the Govern- 
“mént yards could be induced to tke.up the 
study of the classes in this section they 
would benefit immensely, but it,is to ‘be 
feared that their lack - of education in 
the three R's will exclude them from 
participating in the advantages offered 
under this section. Still, they could attend 
the conimercial ‘section’ in the first 
instance and work~ their way up to the 
technical class. 1f employers would only 
encourage their assistanis to improve their 
mental equipment they would be conferring 
aboon on the youths under their control, 
besides helping to advance the interests of 
the Colony. * At the same time, ‘they would 
probably | teap the benefits which flow fom: 
the work of intelligent and educated j juniors. 
There will be two sessions in the year, the 
first beginning in the first week of October 
and closing two weeks before the Chinese 
New Year; and the second commencing 
two weeks after the Chinese New Year 
and closing in. the last week of 
May. “That is to say, the entire winter 
season will be devoted to evening continua. 
tion classes. The classes aro open to all 


In DEFEN Ch OF THe CIVILIAN. 
a 

Whenever.a civilian discovers a grievance 
he immediately assumes that the police’ are 
to blame and promptly decides that theif 
presence in the city is more ornamental than 
useful. * Tt is perfectly true Ubat the police }- 
are occasionally far more interested in some 
coolies who ‘are, passing an idle hour -by 
playing the Chinese equivalent of pitch and 
tass than attending lo more important affairs. 
‘There are those, probably, who would accuse 
the police of being responsible for the sub- 
sidiary coinage nuisance. Hut, after all, the 
police are very much in the hands of theit’ 
superiors nd seldom encouraged | to act 
‘absolutely. on their own. initiative." When 
they do the real character «f the men ap- 
pears, ag was evidenced by the case in which 
Inspector Langley was . prosecutor ‘at .the 
“Magistracy to-day, Asa tevult of that case,. 
which, is reported in. another column, the 
ricksha-coolies will-probably think twice in 
| future before they victimise a civilian! The 
Inspector had’ just- arrived from the New 
Territories, and . being in plain clothes and 
apparently a stranger he appeared to be fair 
game, for the ricksha men, © Whea they |. 
saw him leave the Hongkong - Hotel they 
naturally thought that they had discovered 
a simple tourist, and one bolder than the: 
fest pounced upon . the: Inspector with a 
demand for 80 cents. Now, a tourist ora 
newcomer to the Colony would ‘probably 
have handed, over a few cents to” the’ 
coolie in order to get rid ofthis clamour and 
disturbance. No doubt the coolie reckoned 
upon that, for not only did he yell and 
shout imprecations at the Inspector but he 
also jnduced others of the gang ‘to join in 
the chorift of abuse and calumoy. The In- 
epector stecled his heart against the howling 
dervishes, and if human madre, is & be 
depended, upon at al, Mogheg ‘his alegre

THe body of a Chinese boy was seen float- 
ing in the harbour” early this morning, and 
removed to the mortuary hy the. . police. 
A Chinaman,’ who heard of the recovery 
of the body, attended at the morgue gad’ 
identified the body as that of his son, 
who was drowned'a few days ago during | 
the squall, He said, on being questioned, that 
his son. and ‘himself were rowing for shelter, 
when the squall struck the Colony. A strong. 
wave swept his boy into the sea, He jumped 
into the wa‘er to save him, but the boy had 
already despre 


To- DAY. was an extremely dull day, at the 
Magistracy. With the excepiion of a few 
opium cases and a few petty matters it was a 
day for gamblers. Fourteen men were arrested 
gambling at No. 44, Aberdeen Street,, yester- 
day. The two leaders were fined $15 each 
each and the remainder $2 each, Eight men 
camefrom No. $1, Staunton Street, und they were 
orderéd to pay similar fines, except in the case 
of one, who had a previous conviction, and 
he was mulcted in Sso. West Point came out

Kw AARNE SATURDAY, JULY .28, 1906. 
proval of the members, When the present, 
Viccroy assumed office he was regarded as 
an ardent reformer determined to maintain 
jaw and order, to stamp out the. villainous 
gangs who swoop down on unsuspecting tra- 
ders and harry the defenceless peasantry ;and 
for a time thal idea continued to be held. 
But the energies-of His Excellency failed in 
proportion as the depredations of the out- 
laws increased, until at the present day even 
European vessels, ‘sommanded by European 
\ oficcrs, are not exempt from. the attacks of 
the murderous bands along the ‘coast. “ In’ 
an excellent speech, Mr. D, RK. Law, of 
Messrs, Butterfield & Swire, lamented the 
death of the missionary whose life had been 
spent among. the people, and he. brought 
home to all hearts the ice 
diate action when he said: 
was poor Dr. Macdonald, who was sacrificed 
on the altar of official ineptitude .and cor- 
ruption ; to-day it may be some other valued 
friend ; to-morrow perhaps even yourself if 
your duty calls you to ‘these regions.” 
d observed that if the Chinese Government 
declared its. inability to protect buman life 
on the delta it behoved the British Govern- 
ment in concert with the other Powers whose 
nationals have commercial ‘interests in South 
China, lo put.an end to a “state o' t 
which is a disgrace to civilisat 
Chinese Govetnment has already: admitted 
| its impotence by failing, to expurgate ‘the 

delta districts of criminal gangs, ‘and the 
Htinie is past when ordinary diplomatic repre-

it is possible that such aclion would be’

mainly as the result of the expropriation of 
Tanjong Pagar Docks, is far from satisfactory.

the knowledge that the Colony was not bur-- 
dened with debt of any description, But. a 
the progressive policy of the present Govern- 
or has converted that surplus into an. alerm- 
ing deficit, so that it is' difficult.to see how

FAMOUS PIRATE CHIEFS, 

“ht is a fact recorded in history.that from 
time immemorial and more recently since the 
famous pirate chiefs Ching Yih and Chang 
Pau, with their fleet of about six hundred 
junks, infested’ these coasts, that tributary 
payments were made by trading. craft to 
the robber bands in. order that they might 
The people along 
the coast all ‘paid this toll to-the pirates, 
which showed the fear’thal the gang inspired. 
It is a'matter of history that in the middle of 
the 16th century—in 1551—even according to 
the Chinese themselves, the Imperial forces 
felt no longer possible to crush the pirates, 
No doubt, if we handed over to the robbers 
the money which at ‘present goes into the 
coffers of the Imperial Government we should 
find that our vestels woul: Le tree from the 
matestation to which they have been subjecied 
It would be preposterous 
for us to do such athing. We mast go to the 
root of the evil and exterm 
‘from these coasts."

“Have you arrived at these conclusions from 


rsonal observations ?"” 


 t Well, | have been yiving sou m I

. coustry. Hut, if my views ara notenaugh, con-

untold has been wrought within the Two 
Kwang provinces, due far moye to the fact that

is wus there have been perlogical floods with

ravince and should be capable,

the inhabitants and very many more but for 
| the reason I have advanced—namely, the gam-

In Original Jurisdiction this morning, his 
Honour the Chief Justice, Sir Francis Piggott, 
presiding, delivered judgment in the case in 
which Messrs. Leuba sued Messrs. Ultmann 
and Co., for an injunction to prevent them 
using the “Yeenah" and Po Wai” trade- 
marks on watches sold by the defendant firm. 

His Honour said this action was begun in 
1907, and finished in July of this year, after 
twelve days’ hearing. The plaintiffs’ title de- 
pended in the first place in # grent ‘measure on 
the action or want of action of the Bovets, (the

depends on the action of Ultmane, and a,

under which the rights are acquired, and the 
infringement of them occurs, but we may ex-,

mote familiar ones of the 'aw of torts. In

he is liable far the consequences resulting 
therefrom, if he knew of ils existence, or fore-

the probable consequences. Applied to this

t'daa thing he may do.it, with whatever

SS. "MOLDAVIA" 


.P, & O, LRVIATHAN, ag 


. + 2§th inst, 
* Considerable interest: has attached to the 
arrival of the 38, Afoldavia, in Hongkong, as 
she is’ about ‘the finest vessel the P. and Q. 
«Co. have afloat,’ and expectation: was rife 
to sec how she compared with other leviathans 
of the deep.that-tiave Intely visited this port. 


‘Hitherto it has been in the bands of foreigners: 


to show us'what can be done in tho way of the 


shipbuilder's ast,and how ocean travelling can-}. 


-he made ‘as comfortable as residence in first 
clase hotels, by building what have been claim- 
ed to be “floating hotels,” but now the P. and O. 
iS. N. Co, is apparently waking up tothe fact of 


the keen rivalry and competition in the passen-:, 


ser and cargo-carrying trade between Europe 
andthe Far Enst, with the result that they are 
buiiding boats that can take theit place side by 
side with-anvibing afoat without being com- 
pelled to take second place, One of these 
boats furrived in this harbour for the first'time 
this morning, and was, as was ‘only to be 
expected, subject of much admiring curiosity, 
bot among the craft and their crews afioatand 
the interested members of the shipping ‘and 
mercantile community ‘ashore. As she Jay 
alongside the Kowloon wharf the ss. A7ddavia, 
the new arrival, presented a really magnificent 
appearance, nor was this in any way, detracted 
from on a closer inspection, and a tour 
through the ‘nterior of her iron walls, 
The ss. Afoldavia is a steel, twin-screw, 
steamer, built at Grenock in 1903, and has a 
length of 549 feet over all, with a beam of 58,6, 
and is of 10,090 tons register. Her displace- 
r ment is 15,000 tons, and her average speed is 
19 knots, easily maintained. In all she has six 
| decks, and the boat accommodation, from life- 
boats 10 gigs, will suffice for the supporting: of 
1,000 persons, should occasion arise to require 
Uicir services, while a steam pinnace js kept 
always ready for any possible ‘requirement. 
She is also fitted with troop decks, so. that, st 
a (ew hours’ notice. she, would be ready to con- 
vey troups from one end of the British Empire 
to the other—a_ great desideratum in ‘these’ 
pattous times, As for her accommodation, she 
has berths for gio first class passengeis, and 


150 second, the arranyements being such, by |: 


means of gangways and slides, that each class 
can be entirely cut off from the other, if necés- 
‘and each hold ‘their entertainments, in 

‘own part of the vessel .without in the 
slightest degree interfering with, or embarras- 
sing, the- other: The cabins are fitted with 
electric light and fans, and have large port- 
holes/instead of the usual 6 by 6 inch punctuies 
xenerally met with, Above and circulating the 
saloon, is the music room, which is .fiited up 
in very handsome style, and flanked, fore and 
aft, —with—valuable—oil: paintings,—representing- 
scenes from The Tempest.” Hete are chairs 
and Jaunges to tempt the most miserable 
sufferer from rnal de mer, while a grand piano, 
by ‘Callard and Collard, is ready at all times to 
da service, by. discoursing those sweet strains, 
which are.supposed to (but don’ always) 
“soothe the savage breast.” The saloon, im- 
miediately below, and overlooked all rourd by 
the music’ room, is a handsome: apartment, 
carnble’ of senting 402, persons at a sit- 
ting, and is handsomely panelled in relief 
with, porcelaine de chine, while numerous 
electr.c fans continually: agitate the air and 
help to keep the, atmosphere cool, whén 
necessary.’ Adjoining the saloon is the smoke- 
room, another handsomely upholstered 
apartment, fitted with a bar, and supplied with 
all the paraphernalia for g:mes of sorts, from 
dice to dominoes, and pirguef to poker. The 
floor is covered with rubber-matting ore inch 
thick, and this same rubber is to be found, asa 
snfeguard, wherever passengers might be 
likely. ta- feel to excess the movement of the. 
vessel in bad weather, and: thus be likely to 
Inse their equilibrium. The bathroom. and 
lavatory accommodation is more than ordinarily 
lavish, and such as is not usually found in ordin- 
ity passenger steamers; and what is said of the 
first classaccommodation may,with equaljustice, 
he said of the second class for there isso little to 
choose between them that one marvels “ how it 
can be done at the price.” The promenade 
deck is a magnificent piece of wood-paving, as 
well-lsid and smooth as'if intended, indeed, for 
a ball-room! Dr. Gr fin, an old and well- 
known servant of the company, having seen 
considerable service in the company's fleet, has 
a perfect little hospital, surgery and consulting 
oom, bath-rooms, and isolation room, all ta 
iimeelf and so arranged that.he can at any 
{ime Separate one from the other by means of 
air-tight (and, itiobehoped, germ-tight) doors. , 
The captain, the dector and the purser, are 


all.very well “housed,” but, as usual, much |- 


is felt to be desired in the housiag of the offi- 
cers, upon whom the navigation of the ship, 
"and the safety, welfare and protection of the 
passengers, devolves. Their very ctamped 
and scanty accommodation is perhaps the- one 
defect to be found in this ofherwise very hand- 
some, well-appointéd, and well-faund vessel. 7 


POLICING 7HE WEST RIVER« 
i eee ants 
A VENANG COMMENT. * 


| Referring to the Saénarn piracy case and the 
necessity for policing the West River,. the 
Stratis £ chu saysi:—There is only one craft 
that plys absolutely unmolested on the West 
River and that is the silk junk.’ These are 
heautifully, modelled craft and generally mount 
from seventeen’ to twenty good brass guns and 
carry acrew of from fifty to a hundred men 
‘armed with repeating rifles’ They are fast 
ilers and care for nobody. Of course, China 
is. supposed to police the Wesi River and. she 
does so—after a fashion, Every few miles you 
meet a Chinese guard-boat, generally some old 
junk, armed with antiquated iron guns that are 
tied up in nice little bows of red bunting apd 
which would be more dangerous inboard than 
out if fired. The guard boat. seems to have a 
great predilection for anchoring and swinging 
over her beef bones—only her crew don't eat 
beef—in some out of the way reach, of the river 
where nothing is at all likely to take place, 
Then, too, she keeps a gong’ beating and so 
waras all evil-doers of her presence, with tha 
result that they generally accommodatingly 
carry on their little depredations elsewhere 
rand so Jeave the sleepy river guards In peace,” 
Ifa report is made to the captain of a guard 
boat he geperally finds, Jike the burles ad 
policeman, that itis off his beat, ar he had 
pressing business in the opposite direction 
ow and again one does meet a steam gunboa' 
or terpedo-boat flying the Chinese flag on the 
tiver, but her commander is usually s0 poorly 
aid that he has no time to waste in lookin: 
lor pirates and brigands and so increases hié 
earnings by acting as fow-boat to passenger 
junks that may happen to be ina hurry. It 
thus happens that the policing of the’ West 
River is left. practically in the hands of the 
Treaty Powers and of. these France and Eng- 
fand take the lead with their shallow-draught 
siver gunboats of the, odie and Woodcock 
type. These little crafis are constactly puffing 
up and down the.river, but four! or five boats 
cannot be everywhere at once in a stretch of 
-tiver seyeral hundred mites in extent so the 
pirates, who poisess an excellent intelligence 
department, keep an eye upon thelr movements 
and arrange their attacks s0 as to take place 
durlog thg absgnce of any forgign gunboat,

_ Thave.read with great intérest_a research |:

and Koch, and 1 quite agrea with them, 
that the disease (beri-beri) does not occar 
from micro-organisms. General medical men,

Medical Officer to the Pahang Corporation 
(Quantan Tin Mines, Pahang), informed me 
(hat it’ was owing {o organisms in the 
blood, but i did not agree with their theory 
but held to my opinion that it was. nothing 
More than a sost of pernicious anemia, 1 
acted for Dr. Ralph, the -M:0., who went to. 
Canada on sick leave for six months (from July 
to December),: I kept a record of. the deaths 
of beri-beri cases fur that period, and com- 
} pared it with ‘Dr. Ralph's (from January to 
Jane), He lost +46 cases to my three, My plan 
was, on finding the men attacked with the di- 
Sease, I sent Ihem:. down to the gwala or sea 
side, put them ¢na gencrous diet, and nervine 
tonic, kept ‘hem at the sea-side for a month, 
and most of them recovered to’ return to their 
mining. work. From the experiment J tried 
the renrarks made by Drs, Hunter and Koch 
cotirely coincide with my observations. «

CANTON VIC“ ROV/S TRANSFER PROPOSED. 
A rumour, which while lacking official con- 
firmation, for the present, has every- chance of 
turning out true, bas come from J’eking, says 
the VC. D; News, itis to the effect that the 
Council of State Affairs (Cnéngewuch‘u), after 
frequent secret conferences with the Grand 
Council, is on the point of strongly recommend- 
ting to the Throne the appointment ‘of H. E. 
Tuan Fang (one of the Travelling’ Commis~ 
sioners) at present substantive Viceroy of the 
Min-Ché i Aerial provinces, to be 
Viceroy of the Liangkiang (Kiangsu, Kiangsi 
and Anhui) provinces, in which case the pre-” 
sent acting Viceroy of those provinces, H. E, 
Chou Fu, will go to Canton to take up the sub-. 
stantive Viceroyship of the Two Kwang nro- 
vinces, vice the acting vacumbent H. E. Tsén 
Ch‘un-hsuen, whogoes ta Foochow to fillthe post 
of substantive Viceroy ofthe Min-Ché provinces 
There could not be a happier way’ to satisfy 
/the discontent now rife in the Viceroyalties 
named, the fact being that the present acting 
Viceroy at Nanking is not consideyed in nan 
‘dayin circles to be a strong enough -man for 
the difficult and onerous duties of that post. 
while the notorious squables and disagree- 
ments between Viceroy T+én Ch‘un-hsuen and 
the gentry and merchants at Canton cor.cern- 
ing the Canton-Hankow Railway will be more 
likely to cease. Finally. it is reported that 
H.E. Yang Shih-hsiang at present acting 
Governor of Shaotung province is to be con- 
firmed in his post. At one time there were 
some intentions in Peking to transfer his Ex- 
some other place on account of 
ues against him.

* The Bill passed by the Washington Senate 
on the 18th ult. granting a pension nf $50 (gold) 
amonth toGen. Edward S. Bragg, of ‘Wisconsin, 
was introduced by Senator ooner at the 

feeble health 


upon Congress. 

Gen. Bragg was the author of the famous 
retort ta the criticism of Grover Cleveland 
in the national Democratic convention of 1884; 
“We love Grover Cleveland for-the enemies 
ho hapmade” — ; 3 

In the convention of 1888 he again enme into 
prominence for another saying, While address- 
ing the convention, being of feeble voice, he 
was interrupted by eriew of “Louder.” Finally, 
exasperated by the interruptions, he piped out: 

“When Gabriel. blows his trumpet some 
jackass will call out ‘Louder! ”. 

Gen. Bragg parted company with the De- 
mocratic party in (896 and supported McKinley 
onthe money Issue. We served as Minister to 
Mexico under President Cleveland, was Consul- 
General'to Havana under President McKinley 
abd lster Consul-General to Hongkong. He 
was a delegate to every Democratic national 
convention from 1872 to and including that of
